  "old_string": "We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}\\footnote{Reflexion uses a fixed allocation of 20 episodes per task type ($20 \\times 11 = 220$), vs.\\ the baseline's variable 20--40 per type ($N{=}245$).} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters; both strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\\%$ death rate ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$; 7B: $90.4\\%$), with 5 internal-preferred episodes (vs.\\ 9 at 7B; $2.0\\%$ vs.\\ $3.7\\%$).\nA second 70B-class model, Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct ($N{=}220$), intensifies the pattern: internal death rate $48.6\\%$, deterministic $20.0\\%$ ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +28.6$~pp), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.30$, yielding DAF $= 101.0\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$). DAF exceeding 100\\% indicates that death avoidance more than fully explains the deterministic advantage---when both agents survive, the internal agent performs comparably or slightly better. Label fraction rises to $91.3\\%$ (67 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\nTwo independent 70B-class models thus confirm that $10{\\times}$ scaling does not attenuate death dominance; it intensifies proportional dominance because the deterministic strategy maintains substantially lower death rates than the internal strategy at both 70B scales ($2.75$--$20.0\\%$ vs.\\ $30.6$--$48.6\\%$).",
  "new_string": "We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\\%$ ($[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance."
}

  "old_string": "We examine MATH and ALFWorld as boundary probes (not cross-domain validation). The deterministic oracle differs in information privilege across environments---ground-truth simulator (ScienceWorld), code interpreter (MATH), admissible-action enumeration (ALFWorld; \\S\\ref{sec:problem-setup})---so cross-environment results validate the diagnostic procedure's reusability, not the transferability of specific findings.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$), with 84.4\\% no-preference. Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch, with no death mechanism ($\\delta = 0$, DAF $= 0\\%$). With Qwen2.5-7B, 35 of 37 non-tie labels (94.6\\%) favor the information-advantaged strategy (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env-details}); Llama-3.1-8B (135 games) yields 16 of 16 non-tie labels in the same direction (100\\%). Llama shows fewer non-tie episodes (11.9\\% vs.\\ 25.9\\%) but perfect directional consistency, confirming that information asymmetry, not death avoidance, drives routing signal in ALFWorld regardless of model family. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism (DAF $= 0\\%$ vs.\\ $90.4\\%$) without modification. Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal} summarizes label distributions across all three environments.",
  "new_string": "We probe two environments as boundary cases (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$): no routing signal without catastrophic failure. ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\\%$) shows 94.6\\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy; Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism without modification."
}

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. An implicit router (Qwen3, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA); Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below the majority-vote baseline.\\footnote{The near-chance performance is consistent with noisy-label learning bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning,northcutt2021confident}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative, class imbalance is extreme (90.5\\% deterministic-preferred, 3.7\\% minority), and paraphrase-based text augmentation (25\\%--800\\%) provides no improvement (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-type-out analysis degrades to chance (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), as does a task-type-only classifier ($64.7\\%$ in-distribution, $50.0\\%$ leave-one-task-out (LOTO)), confirming routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$ (95\\% CI: $[-16.33, -5.27]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Results remain inconclusive under the current data regime ($N{=}245$, 3.7\\% minority class).",
  "new_string": "A Qwen3 LoRA router (0.6B--4B; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained on counterfactual labels shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); episode-level evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below majority-vote.\\footnote{Consistent with noisy-label bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative with 90.5\\%/3.7\\% class imbalance (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-out degrades to chance ($49.2\\%$), confirming signal is a per-task-type constant. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$)."
}

97	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
98	\label{sec:daf}
99	
100	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$, where $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} \ll P$ (in our data, $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\approx}\,\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:
101	\begin{equation}
102	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
103	\end{equation}
104	The approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
105	
106	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
107	\begin{equation}

  "old_string": "Since DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).",
  "new_string": "DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\\% CIs)."

_source line 8424 · 2026-05-24T21:00:51.345529+00:00_

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
7	\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\to 1 \Rightarrow r \to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}
8	\label{fig:decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +38.2$\,pp $>$ included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}).
12	
13	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
14	\label{sec:death-decomp}
15	
16	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\%; 95\% CI: $[70.7\%, 87.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
17	
18	DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\% CI$_{\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$, episode-level bootstrap). The 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-invariant characterization (\S\ref{sec:daf}).
19	
20	\paragraph{Per-task structure.}

47	
48	\paragraph{Per-task structure.}
49	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic; all 105 internal deaths are triggered by the \texttt{focus on} action (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task $\Delta$DR correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ at $r = 0.97$ (Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}), a structural consequence of DAF$\to 1$ rather than an independent finding. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} illustrates the typical death trajectory.
50	
51	\begin{figure}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
54	\vspace{-2mm}
55	\caption{Action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} (arrow) appears in final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences (``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'').}
56	\label{fig:death-timeline}

Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\%$) converge within 0.7~pp.

Three architecturally distinct models converge on DAF $88$--$90\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\%$).

68	\label{sec:agent-improvement}

70	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[86.1\%, 97.9\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\%$ ($[95.3\%, 107.5\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.

129	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
130	Eighteen of the 19 excluded task types (95\%) exhibit death-asymmetric patterns (Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}): internal-reasoning death rates range from 40\% to 60\% (mean 53.9\%), while deterministic death rates range from 0\% to 40\% (mean 15.8\%), yielding a mean death-rate differential of $+38.2$\,pp, exceeding the $+28.7$\,pp for included tasks. The included subset is thus \emph{less} death-asymmetric than the excluded subset, the opposite of what a cherry-picking hypothesis would predict. The sole exception is \texttt{grow-plant}, an intrinsically safe task with zero deaths under both strategies, paralleling \texttt{find-non-living-thing} in the included set. The 19 excluded tasks fall into three independently motivated categories: five used during pipeline development (standard hold-out practice), seven taxonomically redundant with included family representatives (deduplication), and seven where the 7B backbone achieves near-zero task progress regardless of strategy (agent capability floor). In no case was a task excluded because death dominance was absent. Exclusion is driven by absence of non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ for 53\% of excluded tasks) and standard deduplication practices. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: zero deaths under both strategies yet included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).

559	
560	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
561	\label{app:failure-modes}
562	
563	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, and applies it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer and triggers the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
564	
565	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task;
…[truncated 3557 chars]

534	Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-env} consolidates replication results across model families and environments.
535	
536	\begin{table}[h]
537	\centering
538	\small
539	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
540	\begin{tabular}{llcrrrccc}
541	\toprule
542	Model & Environment & $n$ & Int DR & Det DR & $\Delta$DR & DAF & DAF 95\% CI & Signal \\
543	\midrule
544	Qwen2.5-7B & ScienceWorld & 245 & 42.9\% & 16.3\% & +26.5 & 90.4\% & [81.3, 99.1] & death \\
545	Llama-3.1-8B & ScienceWorld & 220 & 41.4\% & 11.4\% & +30.0 & 88.2\% & [80.2, 95.1] & death \\
546	Gemma-2-9B-it & ScienceWorld & 220 & 36.4\% & 14.1\% & +22.3 & 89.7\% & [79.5, 98.4] & death \\
547	Qwen2.5-72B & ScienceWorld & 255 & 30.6\% & 2.75\% & +27.8 & 92.4\% & [86.1, 97.9] & death \\
548	Llama-3.1-70B & ScienceWorld & 220 & 48.6\% & 20.0\% & +28.6 & 101.0\% & [95.3, 107.5] & death \\

556	\caption{Cross-model and cross-environment replication summary. \textbf{ScienceWorld}: death dominance replicates across three model families in the 7--9B range and intensifies under $10{\times}$ scaling (two 70B-class models, DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$); DAF 95\% CIs from non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). \textbf{ALFWorld}: DAF $= 0\%$ (no death mechanism); routing signal arises from information asymmetry. \textbf{MATH}: no routing signal without catastrophic failure. Signal column: diagnosed routing mechanism. DR = death rate. Gemma-2's wider CI reflects one zero-death task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}); excluding it yields DAF $= 99.9\%$ $[99.7, 100.0]$.}
557	\label{tab:cross-model-env}
558	\end{table}

_source line 8478 · 2026-05-24T21:06:09.850257+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independen

1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	Methods for augmenting LLMs with tools span self-supervised invocation~\citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, task-level decomposition~\citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based routing~\citep{retool-2025,tool-learning-survey-2024}. All focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?} Concurrent work examines \emph{when} to invoke tools~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026,tool-overuse-illusion-2026}; our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\% of non-tie labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
5	
6	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
7	Adaptive routing ranges from learned halting~\citep{graves2016act} and token-level recursion~\citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} to per-step depth~\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} and multi-turn settings~\citep{mtrouter-2026}; in death-dominated environments, the task-type-level binary signal is structurally inaccessible to these step-level approaches (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}). Model-selection routing~\citep{routellm-2024,frugalgpt-2023,automix-2024} optimizes \emph{which} model to invoke; \citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing under sparse rewards, the closest setting to ours. Unlike token-level adaptive computation~\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024,xin2020deebert} and MoE~\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode granularity.
8	
9	\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation.}
10	Prior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions~\citep{olson2021counterfactual,gajcin2023counterfactual,amitai2024explaining}; recent extensions target LLM agents~\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \emph{before} training. Counterfactual data augmentation~\citep{kaus
…[truncated 1271 chars]

_source line 8518 · 2026-05-24T21:09:39.452052+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property, but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. Six router variants converge on near-chance accuracy ($56$--$59\%$). Two a priori unpredictable results: $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models fails to eliminate death dominance (DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$; Llama-70B exceeds 100\%), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\%$; cross-model DR stability ($36$--$49\%$) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property.
6	
7	\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and practical implications.}
8	Always-deterministic matches 96.3\% of oracle decisions (\S\ref{sec:baselines}), capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points. $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\% of total gap) shows the advantage concentrates almost entirely in death episodes; ALFWorld (DAF${=}0\%$) confirms the framework diagnoses qualitatively different mechanisms. A ${\sim}$50-episode pilot suffices for build-vs-buy decisions (Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19~pp (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The decomposition requires binary catastrophic failures, asymmetric failure rates, and $P \gg \Delta_\text{nd}$---ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH and ALFWorld lack the first.
9	
10

6	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
7	\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}

11	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). 2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]

7	\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
8	\label{fig:label-decomposition}

10	
11	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
12	
13	\subsection{Problem Setup}
14	\label{sec:problem-setup}
15	
16	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \S\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation unce
…[truncated 7293 chars]

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Our episode-level counterfactual framework reveals that 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels in ScienceWorld stem from death avoidance---replicated across three model families and intensifying under $10{\times}$ scaling (DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$ across two independent 70B-class models). Future work should validate this in additional catastrophic-failure settings and investigate targeted episode collection to resolve the router's inconclusive result.
4

36	\section*{Limitations}
37	
38	\paragraph{Environmental scope.}
39	Our primary analysis uses ScienceWorld (11 of 30 task types) with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Cross-model replication (three 7--9B families, two 70B-class models) and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld) support the framework's methodological generality, but quantitative results (DAF $= 90.4\%$, label fractions) are environment-specific. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; softer penalties yield lower values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Validation beyond 70B and to frontier models with substantially lower failure rates remains future work.
40	
41	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}
42	Our internal baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}; more aggressive self-correction (e.g., Tree-of-Thought~\citep{tot-2023}) may further reduce death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, introducing granularity mismatch for individual steps---though only 0.3\% of steps exhibit strict directional conflict with the episode label. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}.

  "new_string": "Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}"
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nMethods for augmenting LLMs with tools span self-supervised invocation~\\citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, task-level decomposition~\\citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based routing~\\citep{retool-2025,tool-learning-survey-2024}. All focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?} Concurrent work examines \\emph{when} to invoke tools~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026,tool-overuse-illusion-2026}; our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\\% of non-tie labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nMethods for augmenting LLMs with tools span self-supervised invocation~\\citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, task-level decomposition~\\citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based routing~\\citep{retool-2025,tool-learning-survey-2024}. Toolformer inserts API calls where they reduce next-token perplexity; ART decomposes novel tasks into tool-augmented subtask programs via in-context retrieval; Chameleon selects heterogeneous modules (web search, code execution, knowledge retrieval) per subtask via an LLM-based planner. All focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?} Concurrent work examines \\emph{when} to invoke tools---\\citet{when2tool-2026} analyze accuracy--latency tradeoffs, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify the computational overhead of tool invocation, and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026,tool-overuse-illusion-2026} investigate tool overuse patterns---but these analyses presuppose that diverse routing signals exist. Our decomposition yields a concrete answer: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of non-tie labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention."
}
```

</details>

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing ranges from learned halting~\\citep{graves2016act} and token-level recursion~\\citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} to per-step depth~\\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} and multi-turn settings~\\citep{mtrouter-2026}; in death-dominated environments, the task-type-level binary signal is structurally inaccessible to these step-level approaches (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}). Model-selection routing~\\citep{routellm-2024,frugalgpt-2023,automix-2024} optimizes \\emph{which} model to invoke; \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing under sparse rewards, the closest setting to ours. Unlike token-level adaptive computation~\\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024,xin2020deebert} and MoE~\\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode granularity.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing ranges from learned halting~\\citep{graves2016act} and token-level recursion~\\citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} to per-step depth adjustment~\\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} and multi-turn model selection~\\citep{mtrouter-2026}. These step-level approaches assume routing signal varies within episodes; in death-dominated environments, the signal is a task-type-level binary property structurally inaccessible to per-step features (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}). Model-selection routing~\\citep{routellm-2024,frugalgpt-2023,automix-2024} optimizes \\emph{which} model to invoke based on query difficulty; \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing under sparse rewards via counterfactual rollouts, the closest setting to ours---though they focus on learning the router rather than diagnosing what signal the router captures. Unlike token-level adaptive computation~\\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024,xin2020deebert} and mixture-of-experts~\\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode granularity."
}
```

</details>

Model-selection routing~\\citep{routellm-2024,frugalgpt-2023,automix-2024} optimizes \\emph{which} model to invoke; \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing under sparse rewards, the closest setting to ours.     "new_string": "\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing ranges from learned halting~\\citep{graves2016act} and token-level recursion~\\citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} to per-step depth adjustment~\\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} and multi-turn model selection~\\citep{mtrouter-2026}. These step-level approaches assume routing signal varies within episodes; in death-dominated environments, the signal is a task-type-level binary property structurally inaccessible to per-step features (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}). Model-selection routing~\\citep{routellm-2024,frugalgpt-2023,automix-2024} optimizes \\emph{which} model to invoke based on query difficulty; \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing under sparse rewards via counterfactual rollouts, the closest setting to ours---though they focus on learning the router rather than diagnosing what signal the router captures. Unlike token-level adaptive computation~\\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024,xin2020deebert} and mixture-of-experts~\\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode granularity."

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions~\\citep{olson2021counterfactual,gajcin2023counterfactual,amitai2024explaining}; recent extensions target LLM agents~\\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \\emph{before} training. Counterfactual data augmentation~\\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs; we execute full counterfactual \\emph{episodes}.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions and measuring outcome changes~\\citep{olson2021counterfactual,gajcin2023counterfactual,amitai2024explaining}; recent extensions target LLM agents via abstract state comparison~\\citep{pona2025abstract}, axis-aligned perturbations~\\citep{axis-2026}, and causal structure discovery~\\citep{cso-2026}. These approaches explain \\emph{why} an agent succeeded or failed on a single trajectory. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \\emph{before} training, answering a different question: what \\emph{type} of signal drives strategy preferences. Counterfactual data augmentation~\\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs to improve model robustness; we execute full counterfactual \\emph{episodes} under controlled strategy variation."
}
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}\nDeath avoidance connects to constrained MDPs~\\citep{altman1999constrained}, safe RL~\\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}, shielding~\\citep{alshiekh2018safe}, and CVaR methods~\\citep{chow2015risk,tamar2015optimizing}; classical methods assume known dynamics, unavailable for LLM agents. \\citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions; SafeRoute~\\citep{saferoute-2025} and Agent-SafetyBench~\\citep{agent-safetybench-2024} address agent safety evaluation. Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified potential outcomes framework~\\citep{rubin1974estimating}, eliminating confounding without structural causal models~\\citep{pearl2009causality}.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}\nDeath avoidance connects to constrained MDPs~\\citep{altman1999constrained}, safe RL~\\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}, shielding~\\citep{alshiekh2018safe}, and CVaR methods~\\citep{chow2015risk,tamar2015optimizing}. Classical methods require known transition dynamics or a differentiable safety model---neither available for black-box LLM agents operating via text interfaces. \\citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions in LLM agents through systematic failure taxonomy; SafeRoute~\\citep{saferoute-2025} and Agent-SafetyBench~\\citep{agent-safetybench-2024} provide benchmarks for agent safety evaluation. Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified potential outcomes framework~\\citep{rubin1974estimating}: by executing both strategies under identical initial conditions, we eliminate confounding without structural causal models~\\citep{pearl2009causality}, at the cost of requiring paired episode collection."
}
```

</details>

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property, but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. Six router variants converge on near-chance accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$). Two a priori unpredictable results: $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models fails to eliminate death dominance (DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$; Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\\%$; cross-model DR stability ($36$--$49\\%$) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and practical implications.}\nAlways-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}), capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points. $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\\% of total gap) shows the advantage concentrates almost entirely in death episodes; ALFWorld (DAF${=}0\\%$) confirms the framework diagnoses qualitatively different mechanisms. A ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot suffices for build-vs-buy decisions (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19~pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The decomposition requires binary catastrophic failures, asymmetric failure rates, and $P \\gg \\Delta_\\text{nd}$---ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH and ALFWorld lack the first.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property, but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories rather than individual observations. Six router variants (0.6B--4B, with and without augmentation) converge on near-chance balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$), and leave-one-task-out evaluation degrades to $49.2\\%$, confirming that the router captures only task-type identity rather than generalizable step-level routing signal.\n\nTwo a priori unpredictable results emerge: $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models fails to eliminate death dominance (DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$; Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\\%$. The cross-model death-rate stability ($36$--$49\\%$ across five models) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property, not a model-specific artifact. This suggests that routing in death-dominated environments is better addressed through action-space constraints (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}) than through learned step-level policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and practical implications.}\nAlways-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}), capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} where perfect per-episode foresight provides negligible benefit over a trivial baseline. This degeneracy arises because $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\\% of total gap): the advantage concentrates almost entirely in death episodes, and the oracle's only meaningful contribution is avoiding the few cases where deterministic performs worse on non-death episodes.\n\nFor practitioners, this suggests a concrete decision procedure: if a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot reveals DAF $> 80\\%$, always-deterministic is likely near-optimal and building a learned router offers minimal return (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}). ALFWorld (DAF${=}0\\%$) confirms the framework diagnoses qualitatively different mechanisms---there, routing signal stems from information asymmetry rather than catastrophic failure, indicating that a learned router may indeed add value.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and applicability conditions.}\nThe decomposition requires three conditions: (i)~binary catastrophic failures with fixed penalty, (ii)~asymmetric failure rates between strategies, and (iii)~$P \\gg \\Delta_\\text{nd}$ so that death dominates the score gap. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH and ALFWorld lack the first. Environments with graded penalties, stochastic failure severity, or symmetric failure rates would require extensions to the DAF framework. Action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19~pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}), suggesting that targeted safety interventions may be more effective than general-purpose routing in death-dominated settings."
}
```

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "Llama-3.1-8B~\\citep{llama3-2024} ($N{=}220$) and Gemma-2-9B-it~\\citep{gemma2-2024} ($N{=}220$) independently replicate death dominance: DAF $= 88.2\\%$ and $89.7\\%$ respectively, with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct models converge on DAF $88$--$90\\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\\%$).",
  "new_string": "Llama-3.1-8B~\\citep{llama3-2024} ($N{=}220$) and Gemma-2-9B-it~\\citep{gemma2-2024} ($N{=}220$) independently replicate death dominance: DAF $= 88.2\\%$ and $89.7\\%$ respectively, with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct model families---Qwen (Alibaba), Llama (Meta), Gemma (Google)---converge on DAF $88$--$90\\%$ with a spread of only 2.2~pp, despite differences in pretraining data, architecture, and tokenization. This narrow convergence suggests that death dominance is an environment-level property rather than a model-specific artifact. Scaling $10{\\times}$ to two 70B-class models (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies rather than attenuates the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\\%$), contrary to the intuition that larger models would reduce the failure gap."
}
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "We probe two environments as boundary cases (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$): no routing signal without catastrophic failure. ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\\%$) shows 94.6\\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy; Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism without modification.",
  "new_string": "We probe two environments as boundary cases to test the framework's specificity (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal reasoning and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$): without a catastrophic failure mechanism, no routing signal emerges despite substantial task difficulty. ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\\%$) shows 94.6\\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy (which enumerates admissible actions); Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism---information asymmetry rather than death avoidance---without modification, confirming that DAF correctly attributes zero signal to death when none exists."
}
```

Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$).",
  "new_string": "A Qwen3 LoRA router (0.6B--4B; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained on counterfactual labels shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$) across all model scales; episode-level evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below majority-vote.\\footnote{Consistent with noisy-label bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative with 90.5\\%/3.7\\% class imbalance (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-out degrades to chance ($49.2\\%$), confirming signal is a per-task-type constant rather than a learnable function of step-level observations. Deployed end-to-end, the best router (4B) scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$); this negative result is consistent with the death-dominance finding, as death avoidance cannot be predicted from pre-episode features when it depends on multi-step trajectory dynamics."
}
```

</details>

[Director 指令] [Patch] 用户反馈三个问题：

**问题 1：Figure 1 位置和引用**
Figure 1（pipeline overview）在 P1，但正文中 `\ref{fig:pipeline}` 零次引用——没有任何文字指向它。

修复：
1. 将 Figure 1 的定义从 introduction.tex 移到 method.tex §3.1 开头（Pipeline 描述的自然位置）
2. 在 method.tex 中首次描述 framework 的段落加入引用："Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates our counterfactual episode collection pipeline..."

**问题 2：正文不够充实（~7 页多）**
需要接近 8 页。方案：

(a) 从附录再提升 1-2 个有叙事价值的表：
- **tab:penalty-sensitivity**（DAF vs penalty magnitude，9 rows × 3 cols）→ 放在 §3.4 DAF 定义段，展示 DAF 对 penalty 的敏感性。用户能直观看到 P=10/50/100 下 DAF 的变化
- **tab:loto-daf**（leave-one-task-out DAF，12 rows × 4 cols）→ 放在 §4.1 或 §4.5 Baselines 段，展示移除任意 task 后 DAF 仍稳健

(b) 恢复之前压缩过度的文本（Related Work、Discussion）

目标：主正文恰好 ~8 页。

**问题 3：配合 Main Agent 修复 Figure 4**
Main Agent 会重画 Figure 4（death_action_timeline），缩短 y-axis 标签并加宽图。你需要确认 caption 在新版本下仍准确。

每步编译验证。

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.tex-417-\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \Delta_{\text{nd}} / \Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$, where $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P) \approx \delta \cdot P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$, $\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). 417	\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \Delta_{\text{nd}} / \Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$, where $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P) \approx \delta \cdot P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$, $\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). Recomputed from 245 episode-level data; bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). \textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}
418	\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}

420	
421	The non-death score gap $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels; it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.
422	
423	\paragraph{Mechanism boundary: death specificity.}
424	The $r = 0.97$ correlation between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$). Broader failure definitions dissolve the correlation entirely: defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score ($T{=}0$) yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$), because non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential between strategies ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$). Table~\ref{tab:penalty-correlation} quantifies this specificity across penalty magni

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}
7	\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
8	\label{fig:label-decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
12	
13	\begin{algorithm}[t]
14	\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}
15	\label{alg:labeling}
16	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
17	\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$
18	\ENSURE Label $\ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref}, {\tt int\text{-}pref}, {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}$
19	\FOR{each $m \in \mathcal{M}$}
20	    \STATE Run episode $\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$
21	    \STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$
22	\ENDFOR
23	\IF{$d_{\tt int} = 1$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 0$}
24	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
25	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = 0$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 1$}
26	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
27	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = d_{\tt det} = 1$}
28	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{both dead}

95	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \texttt{no\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.

100	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$, where $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Binary structure and robustness.}\nAll 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels; only \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) produces non-death signal (SCI $= 0.21$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs: DAF $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$; cluster-robust $[73.3\\%, 100.0\\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\\%$) converge within 0.7~pp.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Binary structure and robustness.}\nAll 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels; only \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) produces non-death signal (SCI $= 0.21$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Leave-one-task-out analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:loto-daf}) confirms robustness: removing any single death-prone task changes DAF by at most 3.0~pp. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs: DAF $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$; cluster-robust $[73.3\\%, 100.0\\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\\%$) converge within 0.7~pp.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nRemoved Task & $N_\\text{rem}$ & DAF (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\n(none, full) & 245 & 78.9 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nFind Non-Living$^\\dagger$ & 205 & 100.0 & +21.1 \\\\\nLifespan Longest & 220 & 75.9 & $-$3.0 \\\\\nLife Stages 1 & 225 & 76.7 & $-$2.2 \\\\\nLifespan Long+Short & 225 & 76.7 & $-$2.2 \\\\\nChemistry Mix & 225 & 77.0 & $-$1.9 \\\\\nGrow Fruit & 225 & 77.3 & $-$1.7 \\\\\nInclined Plane & 225 & 77.3 & $-$1.7 \\\\\nFreeze & 225 & 77.5 & $-$1.4 \\\\\nLife Stages 2 & 225 & 77.5 & $-$1.4 \\\\\nMelt Pt Unknown & 225 & 77.5 & $-$1.4 \\\\\nFind Animal & 225 & 77.8 & $-$1.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Leave-one-task-out DAF sensitivity (label fraction). Removing any death-prone task changes DAF by $\\leq$3.0\\,pp. $\\dagger$\\,Sole death-free task; removal raises DAF to 100\\% by construction.}\n\\label{tab:loto-daf}\n\\end{table}"
}

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independen

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}
7	\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
8	\label{fig:label-decomposition}
9	\end{figure}

11	Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.

    "old_string": "Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}",
    "new_string": "Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}"

120	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
121	
122	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[86.1\%, 97.9\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\%$ ($[95.3\%, 107.5\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.
123	
124	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
125	We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\% \to 43.5\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\sim}8$~pp at 80\% power; Appendix~\ref{app:dose-response}).
126	
127	\begin{figure}[t]
128	\centering
129	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}
130	\caption{Death rate vs.\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\% CIs.}
131	\label{fig:dose-response}
132	\end{figure}

_source line 8684 · 2026-05-25T08:00:04.127713+00:00_

168	\label{tab:baselines}
169	\end{table}
170	
171	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}). Random routing ($-22.38$) falls between the two pure strategies, confirming that the gap is not an artifact of evaluation methodology. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \emph{oracle degeneracy} where perfect per-episode foresight provides marginal benefit over the trivial always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).
172	
173	\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}
174	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
175	
176	A Qwen3 LoRA router (0.6B--4B; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained on counterfactual labels shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$) across all model scales; episode-level evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below majority-vote.\footnote{Consistent with noisy-label bounds~\citep{natarajan2013learning}: ${\sim}$88\% of step-level labels are uninformative with 90.5\%/3.7\% class imbalance (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-out degrades to chance ($49.2\%$), confirming signal is a per-task-type constant rather than a learnable function of step-level observations. Deployed end-to-end, the best router (4B) scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$); this negative result is consistent with the death-dominance finding, as death avoidance cannot be predicted from pre-episode features when it depends on multi-step trajectory dynamics.
177

_source line 8714 · 2026-05-25T08:03:22.838697+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independen
…[truncated 2789 chars]

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
7	\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\to 1 \Rightarrow r \to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}
8	\label{fig:decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +38.2$\,pp $>$ included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}).
12	
13	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
14	\label{sec:death-decomp}
15	
16	\begin{table}[t]
17	\centering
18	\scriptsize
19	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}
20	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrccc@{}}
21	\toprule
22	Task & $n$ & $\Delta_\text{full}$ & D\%\textsubscript{int} & D\%\textsubscript{det} & D / I / N \\
23	\midrule
24	id-life-stages-1       & 20  & $+47.50$ & 55   & 10   & 9 / 0 / 11  \\
25	lifespan-long          & 25  & $+44.00$ & 56   & 16   & 11 / 1 / 13 \\
26	lifespan-l-then-s      & 20  & $+40.80$ & 45   & 10   & 8 / 1 / 11  \\

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
6	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
7	\label{fig:pipeline}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
11	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
12	
13	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
14	
15	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactua
